<span class="atom-split" data-atom="king_john" data-title="KING JOHN" data-chapter="" data-kind="intro"></span>


[[King John|KING JOHN]]
A PLAY called The Troublesome Reign of John, [[King]] of England, published anonymously in 1591, has sometimes been thought to be a derivative version of Shakespeare’s [[King John]], first published in the 1623 Folio; more probably Shakespeare wrote his play in 1595 or 1596, using The Troublesome Reign—itself based on Holinshed’s Chronicles and John Foxe’s Book of Martyrs (1563)—as his principal source. Like [[Richard II]], [[King John]] is written entirely in verse.
[[King John]] (c.1167―1216) was famous as the opponent of papal tyranny, and The Troublesome Reign is a violently anti-Catholic play; but Shakespeare is more moderate. He portrays selected events from John’s reign—like The Troublesome Reign, making no mention of Magna Carta—and ends with John’s [[Death|death]], but John is not so dominant a figure in his play as [[Richard II]] or [[Richard III]] in theirs. Indeed, the longest—and liveliest—role is that of [[Richard]] Coeur-de-[[Lion|lion]]’s illegitimate son, Philip Falconbridge, the Bastard.
[[King John]]’s reign was troublesome initially because of his weak claim to his brother [[Richard]] Coeur-de-[[Lion|lion]]’s throne. Prince [[Arthur]], son of John’s elder brother Geoffrey, had no less strong a claim, which is upheld by his mother, [[Constance]], and by [[King Philip of France]]. The waste and futility of the consequent war between power-hungry leaders is satirically demonstrated in the dispute over the French town of Angers, which is resolved by a [[Marriage|marriage]] between John’s niece, Lady Blanche of Spain, and Louis, the French [[Dauphin]]. The moral is strikingly drawn by the Bastard—the man best fitted to be [[King|king]], but debarred by accident of birth—in his speech (2.1.562-99) on ’commodity’ ([[Self|self]]-interest). [[King]] Philip breaks his treaty with England, and in the ensuing battle Prince [[Arthur]] is captured. He becomes the play’s [[Touchstone|touchstone]] of humanity as he persuades John’s agent, [[Hubert]], to disobey John’s orders to blind him, only to kill himself while trying to escape. John’s noblemen, thinking the [[King]] responsible for the boy’s [[Death|death]], defect to the French, but return to their allegiance on learning that the [[Dauphin]] intends to kill them after conquering England. John dies, poisoned by a monk; the play ends with the reunited noblemen swearing allegiance to John’s son, the young Henry III, and with the Bastard’s boast that
This England never did, nor never shall,
Lie at the proud foot of a conqueror
But when it first did help to wound itself.
Twentieth-century revivals of [[King John]] were infrequent, but it was popular in the nineteenth century, when the roles of the [[King]], the Bastard, and [[Constance]] all appealed to successful actors; a production of 1823 at Covent [[Garden]] inaugurated a trend for historically accurate settings and costumes which led to a number of spectacular revivals.
THE PERSONS OF THE PLAY
[[King John|KING JOHN]] of England
[[Queen|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor|ELEANOR]], his mother

LADY FALCONBRIDGE
[[Philip the Bastard|Philip the BASTARD]], later knighted as Sir [[Richard]] Plantagenet,
her illegitimate son by [[King]] [[Richard]] I (Coeur-de-[[Lion|lion]])
Robert FALCONBRIDGE, her legitimate son
James GURNEY, her attendant

Lady BLANCHE of Spain, niece of [[King John]]
PRINCE HENRY, son of [[King John]]
[[Hubert|HUBERT]], a follower of [[King John]]
[[Earl of Salisbury|Earl of SALISBURY]]
Earl of PEMBROKE
Earl of ESSEX
Lord BIGOT
[[King Philip of France|KING PHILIP of France]]
LOUIS THE [[Dauphin|DAUPHIN]], his son
[[Arthur|ARTHUR]], Duke of Brittaine, nephew of [[King John]]
Lady [[Constance|coNSTANCE]], his mother
Duke of AUSTRIA (Limoges)
CHÂTILLON, ambassador from France to England
Count MELUN

A CITIZEN of Angers
Cardinal PANDOLF, a legate from the Pope
PETER OF POMFRET, a [[Prophet|prophet]]
HERALDS
EXECUTIONERS
MESSENGERS

Lords, soldiers, attendants

The Life and [[Death]] of [[King John]]

1.1 [Flourish.] Enter [[King John]], [[Queen]] [[Eleanor]], and the Earls of Pembroke, Essex, and Salisbury; with them Châtillon of France
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Now say, Châtillon, what would France with us?
CHÂTILLON
Thus, after greeting, speaks the [[King of France]],
In my behaviour, to the majesty—
The borrowed majesty—of England here.
[[Queen|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor|ELEANOR]]
A strange beginning: ‘borrowed majesty’?
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
[[Silence]], good mother, hear the embassy.
CHÂTILLON
Philip of France, in right and true behalf
Of thy deceased brother Geoffrey’s son,
[[Arthur]] Plantagenet, lays most lawful claim

To this fair island and the territories,
To Ireland, Poitou, Anjou, Touraine, Maine;
Desiring thee to lay aside the [[Sword|sword]]
Which sways usurpingly these several titles,
And put the same into young [[Arthur]]’s hand,
Thy nephew and right royal sovereign.
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
What follows if we disallow of this?
CHÂTILLON
The proud control of fierce and bloody war,
To enforce these rights so forcibly withheld—
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Here have we war for war, and [[Blood|blood]] for [[Blood|blood]],
Controlment for controlment: so answer France.
CHÂTILLON
Then take my [[King|king]]’s defiance from my mouth,
The farthest limit of my embassy.
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Bear mine to him, and so depart in peace.
Be thou as [[Lightning|lightning]] in the eyes of France,
For ere thou canst report, I will be there; 25
The thunder of my cannon shall be heard.
So hence. Be thou the trumpet of our wrath,
And sullen presage of your own [[Decay|decay]].—
An honourable conduct let him have;
Pembroke, look to’t.—Farewell, Châtillon. 30
Exeunt Châtillon and Pembroke
[[Queen|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor|ELEANOR]]
What now, my son? Have I not ever said
How that ambitious [[Constance]] would not cease
Till she had kindled France and all the world
Upon the right and party of her son ?
This might have been prevented and made whole 35
With very easy arguments of [[Love|love]],
Which now the manage of two kingdoms must
With fearful-bloody issue arbitrate.
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Our strong possession and our right for us.
[[Queen|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor|ELEANOR]] (aside to [[King John]])
Your strong possession much more than your right,
Or else it must go wrong with you and me: 41
So much my [[Conscience|conscience]] whispers in your ear,
Which none but heaven and you and I shall hear.
Enter a Sheriff, [who whispers to Essex]
ESSEX
My liege, here is the strangest controversy,
Come from the country to be judged by you, 45
That e’er I heard. Shall I produce the men?
[[King John|KING JOHN]] Let them approach.—⌈Exit Sheriff⌉
Our abbeys and our priories shall pay
This expeditious charge.
Enter Robert Falconbridge and [[Philip the Bastard]] ⌈With
the
Sheriff⌉
What men are you?
BASTARD
Your faithful subject I, a gentleman 50
Born in Northamptonshire, and eldest son,
As I suppose, to Robert Falconbridge,
A soldier, by the [[Honour|honour]]-giving hand
Of Cœur-de-[[Lion|lion]] knighted in the field.
[[King John|KING JOHN]] What art thou? 55
FALCONBRIDGE
The son and heir to that same Falconbridge.
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Is that the elder, and art thou the heir?
You came not of one mother then, it seems.
BASTARD
Most certain of one mother, mighty [[King]]—
That is [[Well|well]] known—and, as I think, one father. 60
But for the certain knowledge of that [[Truth|truth]]
I put you o’er to heaven, and to my mother.
Of that I [[Doubt|doubt]] as all men’s children may.
[[Queen|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor|ELEANOR]]
Out on thee, rude man ! Thou dost shame thy mother
And wound her [[Honour|honour]] with this diffidence. 65
BASTARD
I, Madam? No, I have no reason for it.
That is my brother’s plea and none of mine,
The which if he can prove, a pops me out
At least from fair five hundred pound a year.
Heaven guard my mother’s [[Honour|honour]], and my land! 70
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
A good blunt fellow.—Why, being younger born,
Doth he lay claim to thine inheritance ?
BASTARD
I know not why, except to get the land;
But once he slandered me with bastardy.
But whe’er I be as true begot or no, 75
That still I lay upon my mother’s head;
But that I am as [[Well|well]] begot, my liege—
Fair fall the bones that took the pains for me—
Compare our faces and be judge yourself.
If old Sir Robert did beget us both 80
And were our father, and this son like him,
O old Sir Robert, father, on my knee
I give heaven thanks I was not like to thee.
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Why, what a madcap hath heaven lent us here!
[[Queen|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor|ELEANOR]]
He hath a trick of Coeur-de-[[Lion|lion]]’s face; 85
The accent of his tongue affecteth him.
Do you not read some tokens of my son
In the large composition of this man?
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Mine eye hath [[Well|well]] examined his parts,
And finds them perfect [[Richard]].
(To Robert Falconbridge) Sirrah, speak: 90
What doth move you to claim your brother’s land?
BASTARD
Because he hath a half-face like my father!
With half that face would he have all my land,
A half-faced groat five hundred pound a year.
FALCONBRIDGE
My gracious liege, when that my father lived, 95
Your brother did employ my father much—
BASTARD
[[Well]], sir, by this you cannot get my land.
Your tale must be how he employed my mother.
FALCONBRIDGE
And once dispatched him in an embassy
To [[Germany]], there with the Emperor
To treat of high affairs touching that [[Time|time]].
Th‘advantage of his absence took the [[King]],
And in the meantime sojourned at my father’s,
Where how he did prevail I shame to speak.
But [[Truth|truth]] is [[Truth|truth]]:large lengths of seas and shores
Between my father and my mother lay, 106
As I have heard my father speak himself,
When this same lusty gentleman was got.
Upon his deathbed he by will bequeathed
His lands to me, and took it on his [[Death|death]] 110
That this my mother’s son was none of his;
And if he were, he came into the world
Full fourteen weeks before the course of [[Time|time]].
Then, good my liege, let me have what is mine,
My father’s land, as was my father’s will. 115
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Sirrah, your brother is legitimate.
Your father’s wife did after wedlock bear him,
And if she did play false, the fault was hers,
Which fault lies on the hazards of all husbands
That marry wives. Tell me, how if my brother,
Who, as you say, took pains to get this son,
Had of your father claimed this son for his ?
In sooth, good friend, your father might have kept
This calf, bred from his cow, from all the world;
In sooth he might. Then if he were my brother’s, 125
My brother might not claim him, nor your father,
Being none of his, refuse him. This concludes:
My mother’s son did get your father’s heir;
Your father’s heir must have your father’s land.
FALCONBRIDGE
Shall then my father’s will be of no force 130
To dispossess that [[Child|child]] which is not his?
BASTARD
Of no more force to dispossess me, sir,
Than was his will to get me, as I think.
[[Queen|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor|ELEANOR]]
Whether hadst thou rather be: a Falconbridge,
And like thy brother to enjoy thy land, 135
Or the reputed son of Cœur-de-[[Lion|lion]],
Lord of thy presence, and no land beside?
BASTARD
Madam, an if my brother had my shape,
And I had his, Sir Robert’s his like him,
And if my legs were two such riding-rods, 140
My arms such eel-skins stuffed, my face so thin
That in mine ear I durst not stick a [[Rose|rose]]
Lest men should say ‘Look where three-farthings
goes!’,
And, to his shape, were heir to all this land,
Would I might never stir from off this place.
I would give it every foot to have this face;
It would not be Sir Nob in any case.
[[Queen|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor|ELEANOR]]
I like thee [[Well|well]]. Wilt thou forsake thy fortune,
Bequeath thy land to him, and follow me?
I am a soldier and now bound to France. 150
BASTARD
Brother, take you my land; I’ll take my chance.
Your face hath got five hundred pound a year,
Yet sell your face for fivepence and ’tis dear.—
Madam, I’ll follow you unto the [[Death|death]].
[[Queen|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor|ELEANOR]]
Nay, I would have you go before me thither. 155
BASTARD
Our country manners give our betters way.
[[King John|KING JOHN]] What is thy name?
BASTARD
Philip, my liege, so is my name begun:
Philip, good old Sir Robert’s wife’s eldest son,
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
From henceforth bear his name whose form thou
bear’st. 160
Kneel thou down Philip, but arise more great:
He knights the Bastard
Arise Sir [[Richard]] and Plantagenet.
BASTARD
Brother by th’ mother’s side, give me your hand.
My father gave me [[Honour|honour]], yours gave land.
Now blessèd be the hour, by [[Night|night]] or day, 165
When I was got, Sir Robert was away.
[[Queen|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor|ELEANOR]]
The very spirit of Plantagenet I
I am thy grandam, [[Richard]]; call me so.
BASTARD
Madam, by chance, but not by [[Truth|truth]]; what though?
Something about, a little from the right,
In at the [[Window|window]], or else o‘er the hatch;
Who dares not stir by day must walk by [[Night|night]],
And have is have, however men do catch.
Near or far off, [[Well|well]] won is still [[Well|well]] shot,
And I am I, howe’er I was begot.
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Go, Falconbridge, now hast thou thy [[Desire|desire]]:
A landless knight makes thee a landed squire.—
Come, madam, and come, [[Richard]]; we must [[Speed|speed]]
For France; for France, for it is more than need.
BASTARD
Brother, adieu. Good fortune come to thee, 180
For thou wast got i’th’ way of honesty.
Exeunt all but the Bastard
A foot of [[Honour|honour]] better than I was,
But many a many foot of land the worse.
[[Well]], now can I make any Joan a lady.
‘Good e’en, Sir [[Richard]]‘—’God-a-[[Mercy|mercy]] fellow’;
And if his name be George I’ll call him Peter,
For new-made [[Honour|honour]] doth forget men’s names;
’Tis too respective and too sociable
For your [[conversion]]. Now your traveller,
He and his toothpick at my worship’s mess; 190
And when my knightly stomach is sufficed,
Why then I suck my teeth and catechize
My picked man of countries. ‘My dear sir,’
Thus leaning on mine elbow I begin,
‘I shall beseech you—’. That is Question now; 195
And then comes Answer like an Absey book.
‘O sir,’ says Answer, ‘at your best command,
At your employment, at your service, sir.’
‘No sir,’ says Question, ‘I, sweet sir, at yours.’
And so, ere Answer knows what Question would,
Saving in dialogue of compliment,
And talking of the Alps and Apennines,
The Pyrenean and the [[River]] Po,
It draws toward supper in conclusion so.
But this is worshipful society, 205
And fits the mounting spirit like myself;
For he is but a bastard to the [[Time|time]]
That doth not smack of observation;
And so am I—whether I smack or no,
And not [[Alone|alone]] in habit and device, 210
Exterior form, outward accoutrement,
But from the inward motion—to deliver
Sweet, sweet, sweet [[Poison|poison]] for the age’s tooth;
Which, though I will not practise to deceive,
Yet to avoid deceit I mean to learn; 215
For it shall strew the footsteps of my rising.
Enter Lady Falconbridge and James Gurney
But who comes in such haste in riding-robes?
What woman-post is this? Hath she no husband
That will take pains to blow a horn before her?
O me, ’tis my mother! How now, good lady? 220
What brings you here to [[Court|court]] so hastily?
LADY FALCONBRIDGE
Where is that slave thy brother? Where is he
That holds in chase mine [[Honour|honour]] up and down?
BASTARD
My brother Robert, old Sir Robert’s son?
Colbrand the Giant, that same mighty man? 225
Is it Sir Robert’s son that you seek so?
LADY FALCONBRIDGE
Sir Robert’s son, ay, thou unreverent boy,
Sir Robert’s son. Why scorn’st thou at Sir Robert?
He is Sir Robert’s son, and so art thou.
BASTARD
James Gurney, wilt thou give us leave awhile? 230
GURNEY
Good leave, good Philip.
BASTARD Philip Sparrow, James!
There’s toys abroad; anon I’ll tell thee more.
Exit James Gurney

Madam, I was not old Sir Robert’s son.
Sir Robert might have eat his part in me
Upon Good Friday, and ne’er broke his fast. 235
Sir Robert could do [[Well|well]], marry to confess,
Could a get me! Sir Robert could not do it:
We know his handiwork. Therefore, good mother,
To whom am I beholden for these limbs?
Sir Robert never holp to make this leg. 240
LADY FALCONBRIDGE
Hast thou conspired with thy brother too,
That for thine own gain shouldst defend mine [[Honour|honour]]?
What means this scorn, thou most untoward knave?
BASTARD
Knight, knight, good mother, Basilisco-like!
What! I am dubbed; I have it on my shoulder. 245
But, mother, I am not Sir Robert’s son.
I have disclaimed Sir Robert; and my land,
Legitimation, name, and all is gone.
Then, good my mother, let me know my father;
Some proper man, I [[Hope|hope]]; who was it, mother? 250
LADY FALCONBRIDGE
Hast thou denied thyself a Falconbridge ?
BASTARD
As faithfully as I deny the devil.
LADY FALCONBRIDGE
[[King]] [[Richard]] Cœur-de-[[Lion|lion]] was thy father.
By long and vehement suit I was seduced
To make room for him in my husband’s bed. 255
Heaven lay not my transgression to my charge!
Thou art the issue of my dear offence,
Which was so strongly urged past my defence.
BASTARD
Now by this light, were I to get again,
Madam, I would not wish a better father. 260
Some sins do bear their privilege on earth,
And so doth yours; your fault was not your folly.
Needs must you lay your heart at his dispose,
Subjected tribute to commanding [[Love|love]],
Against whose fury and unmatched force 265
The aweless [[Lion|lion]] could not wage the fight,
Nor keep his princely heart from [[Richard]]’s hand.
He that perforce robs lions of their hearts
May easily win a woman’s. Ay, my mother,
With all my heart I thank thee for my father. 270
Who lives and dares but say thou didst not [[Well|well]]
When I was got, I’ll send his [[Soul|soul]] to hell.
Come, lady, I will show thee to my kin,
And they shall say, when [[Richard]] me begot,
If thou hadst said him nay, it had been sin. 275
Who says it was, he lies: I say ’twas not. Exeunt

2.1 ⌈Flourish.⌉ Enter before Angers ⌈at one door⌉ Philip [[King of France]], Louis the [[Dauphin]], Lady [[Constance]], and [[Arthur]] Duke of Brittaine, with soldiers; ⌈at another door⌉ the Duke of Austria, wearing a [[Lion|lion]]’s hide, with soldiers
⌈[[King|KING]] PHILIP⌉
Before Angers [[Well|well]] met, brave Austria.—
[[Arthur]], that great forerunner of thy [[Blood|blood]],
[[Richard]] that robbed the [[Lion|lion]] of his heart
And fought the holy wars in Palestine,
By this brave duke came early to his [[Grave|grave]]; 5
And, for amends to his posterity,
At our importance hither is he come
To spread his colours, boy, in thy behalf,
And to rebuke the usurpation
Of thy unnatural uncle, English John. 10
Embrace him, [[Love|love]] him, give him welcome hither.
[[Arthur|ARTHUR]] (to Austria)
God shall forgive you Cœur-de-[[Lion|lion]]’s [[Death|death]],
The rather that you give his offspring life,
Shadowing their right under your wings of war.
I give you welcome with a powerless hand, 15
But with a heart full of unstained [[Love|love]].
Welcome before the gates of Angers, Duke.
⌈[[King|KING]] PHILIP⌉
A noble boy. Who would not do thee right?
AUSTRIA (kissing [[Arthur]])
Upon thy cheek lay I this zealous kiss
As seal to this indenture of my [[Love|love]]: 20
That to my [[Home|home]] I will no more return
Till Angers and the right thou hast in France,
Together with that pale, that white-faced shore,
Whose foot spurns back the ocean’s roaring tides
And coops from other lands her islanders, 25
Even till that England, hedged in with the main,
That [[Water|water]]-wallèd bulwark, still secure
And confident from foreign purposes,
Even till that utmost corner of the west
Salute thee for her [[King|king]]. Till then, fair boy,
Will I not think of [[Home|home]], but follow arms.
C0NSTANCE
O, take his mother’s thanks, a widow’s thanks,
Till your strong hand shall help to give him strength
To make a more requital to your [[Love|love]].
AUSTRIA
The peace of heaven is theirs that lift their swords
In such a just and charitable war.
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
[[Well]] then, to work! Our cannon shall be bent
Against the brows of this resisting town.
Call for our chiefest men of discipline
To cull the plots of best advantages.
We’ll lay before this town our royal bones,
Wade to the market-place in Frenchmen’s [[Blood|blood]],
But we will make it subject to this boy.
[[Constance|CONSTANCE]]
Stay for an answer to your embassy,
Lest unadvised you stain your swords with [[Blood|blood]].
My lord Châtillon may from England bring
That right in peace which here we urge in war,
And then we shall repent each drop of [[Blood|blood]]
That hot rash haste so indirectly shed.
Enter Châtillon
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
A [[Wonder|wonder]], lady:lo upon thy wish
Our messenger Châtillon is arrived.—
What England says, say briefly, gentle lord;
We coldly pause for thee. Châtillon, speak.
CHÂTILLON
Then turn your forces from this paltry siege,
And stir them up against a mightier task. 55
England, impatient of your just demands,
Hath put himself in arms. The adverse winds,
Whose leisure I have stayed, have given him [[Time|time]]
To land his legions all as soon as I.
His marches are expedient to this town, 60
His forces strong, his soldiers confident.
With him along is come the Mother-[[Queen]],
An Ate stirring him to [[Blood|blood]] and strife;
With her her niece, the Lady Blanche of Spain;
With them a bastard of the [[King]]’s deceased; 65
And all th‘unsettled humours of the land—
Rash, inconsiderate, fiery voluntaries,
With ladies’ faces and fierce dragons’ spleens—
Have sold their fortunes at their native homes,
Bearing their birthrights proudly on their backs, 70
To make a hazard of new fortunes here.
In brief, a braver choice of dauntless spirits
Than now the English bottoms have waft o’er
Did never float upon the swelling tide
To do offence and scathe in Christendom. 75
Drum beats
The interruption of their churlish drums
Cuts off more circumstance. They are at hand;
To parley or to fight therefore prepare.
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
How much unlooked-for is this expedition I
AUSTRIA
By how much unexpected, by so much 80
We must awake endeavour for defence,
For [[Courage|courage]] mounteth with occasion.
Let them be welcome then: we are prepared.
Enter, ⌈marching,⌉ [[King John]] of England, the Bastard, [[Queen]] [[Eleanor]], Lady Blanche, the Earl of Pembroke, and soldiers
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Peace be to France, if France in peace permit
Our just and lineal entrance to our own. 85
If not, bleed France, and peace ascend to heaven,
Whiles we, God’s wrathful agent, do correct
Their proud contempt that beats his peace to heaven.
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
Peace be to England, if that war return
From France to England, there to live in peace.
England we [[Love|love]], and for that England’s sake
With burden of our armour here we sweat.
This toil of ours should be a work of thine;
But thou from loving England art so far
That thou hast underwrought his lawful [[King|king]], 95
Cut off the sequence of posterity,
Outfaced infant state, and done a rape
Upon the maiden virtue of the [[Crown|crown]].
(Pointing to [[Arthur]])
Look here upon thy brother Geoffrey’s face.
These eyes, these brows, were moulded out of his; 100
This little abstract doth contain that large
Which died in Geoffrey; and the hand of [[Time|time]]
Shall draw this brief into as huge a volume.
That Geoffrey was thy elder brother born,
And this his son; England was Geoffrey’s right, 105
And this is Geoffrey’s. In the name of God,
How comes it then that thou art called a [[King|king]],
When living [[Blood|blood]] doth in these temples beat,
Which owe the [[Crown|crown]] that thou o’ermasterest?
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
From whom hast thou this great commission, France,
To draw my answer from thy articles? 111
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
From that supernal judge that stirs good thoughts
In any breast of strong authority
To look into the blots and stains of right.
That judge hath made me guardian to this boy, 115
Under whose warrant I impeach thy wrong,
And by whose help I mean to chastise it.
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Alack, thou dost usurp authority.
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
Excuse it is to beat usurping down.
[[Queen|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor|ELEANOR]]
Who is it thou dost call [[Usurper|usurper]], France? 120
[[Constance|CONSTANCE]]
Let me make answer: thy usurping son.
[[Queen|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor|ELEANOR]]
Out, insolent! Thy bastard shall be [[King|king]]
That thou mayst be a [[Queen|queen]] and check the world.
[[Constance|CONSTANCE]]
My bed was ever to thy son as true
As thine was to thy husband; and this boy 125
Liker in feature to his father Geoffrey
Than thou and John in manners, being as like
As rain to [[Water|water]], or devil to his dam.
My boy a bastard? By my [[Soul|soul]] I think
His father never was so true begot. 130
It cannot be, an if thou wert his mother.
[[Queen|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor|ELEANOR]] (to [[Arthur]])
There’s a good mother, boy, that blots thy father.
[[Constance|CONSTANCE]] (to [[Arthur]])
There’s a good grandam, boy, that would blot thee.
AUSTRIA
Peace!
BASTARD Hear the crier!
AUSTRIA What the devil art thou?
BASTARD
One that will play the devil, sir, with you, 135
An a may catch your hide and you [[Alone|alone]].
You are the hare of whom the proverb goes,
Whose valour plucks dead lions by the beard.
I’ll smoke your skin-coat an I catch you right—
Sirrah, look to’t—i’[[Faith|faith]] I will, i’[[Faith|faith]]!! 140
BLANCHE
O, [[Well|well]] did he become that [[Lion|lion]]’s robe
That did disrobe the [[Lion|lion]] of that robe!
BASTARD
It lies as sightly on the back of him
As great Alcides’ shows upon an ass.
But, ass, I’ll take that burden from your back, 145
Or lay on that shall make your shoulders crack.
AUSTRIA
What cracker is this same that deafs our ears
With this abundance of superfluous breath ?—
[[King]] Philip, determine what we shall do straight.
⌈[[King|KING]] PHILIPI⌉
Women and fools, break off your conference.—150
[[King John]], this is the very sum of all:
England and Ireland, Anjou, Touraine, Maine,
In right of [[Arthur]] do I claim of thee.
Wilt thou resign them and lay down thy arms?
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
My life as soon. I do defy thee, France.—155
[[Arthur]] of Brittaine, yield thee to my hand,
And out of my dear [[Love|love]] I’ll give thee more
Than e’er the coward hand of France can win.
Submit thee, boy.
[[Queen|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor|ELEANOR]] (to [[Arthur]]) Come to thy grandam, [[Child|child]].
[[Constance|CONSTANCE]] (to [[Arthur]])
Do, [[Child|child]], go to it grandam, [[Child|child]]. 160
Give grandam kingdom, and it grandam will
Give it a plum, a cherry, and a fig.
There’s a good grandam.
[[Arthur|ARTHUR]] Good my mother, peace.
I would that I were low laid in my [[Grave|grave]].
I am not worth this coil that’s made for me. 165
He weeps
[[Queen|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor|ELEANOR]]
His mother shames him so, poor boy, he weeps.
[[Constance|CONSTANCE]]
Now shame upon you, whe’er she does or no !
His grandam’s wrongs, and not his mother’s shames,
Draw those heaven-moving pearls from his poor eyes,
Which heaven shall take in [[Nature|nature]] of a fee; 170
Ay, with these crystal beads heaven shall be bribed
To do him justice and [[Revenge|revenge]] on you.
[[Queen|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor|ELEANOR]]
Thou monstrous slanderer of heaven and earth !
[[Constance|CONSTANCE]]
Thou monstrous injurer of heaven and earth I
Call not me slanderer. Thou and thine usurp
The dominations, royalties and rights
Of this oppressed boy. This is thy eld’st son’s son,
Infortunate in nothing but in thee.
Thy sins are visited in this poor [[Child|child]];
The canon of the law is laid on him,
Being but the second generation
Removed from thy sin-conceiving womb.
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Bedlam, have done.
[[Constance|CONSTANCE]] I have but this to say:
That he is not only plagued for her sin,
But God hath made her sin and her the plague 185
On this removed issue, plagued for her
And with her plague; her sin his injury,
Her injury the beadle to her sin;
All punished in the person of this [[Child|child]],
And all for her. A plague upon her! 190
[[Queen|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor|ELEANOR]]
Thou unadvised scold, I can produce
A will that bars the title of thy son.
[[Constance|CONSTANCE]]
Ay, who doubts that? A will, a wicked will,
A woman’s will, a cankered grandam’s will!
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
Peace, lady; pause or be more temperate.
It ill beseems this presence to cry aim
To these ill-tunèd repetitions.—
Some trumpet summon hither to the walls
These men of Angers. Let us hear them speak
Whose title they admit, [[Arthur]]’s or John’s. 200
Trumpet sounds. Enter a Citizen upon the walls
CITIZEN
Who is it that hath warned us to the walls?
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
’Tis France for England.
[[King John|KING JOHN]] England for itself.
You men of Angers and my loving subjects—
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
You loving men of Angers, [[Arthur]]’s subjects,
Our trumpet called you to this gentle parle—205
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
For our advantage; therefore hear us first.
These flags of France that are advanced here
Before the eye and prospect of your town,
Have hither marched to your endamagement.
The cannons have their bowels full of wrath, 210
And ready mounted are they to spit forth
Their iron indignation ’gainst your walls.
All preparation for a bloody siege
And merciless proceeding by these French
Confront your [[City|city]]’s eyes, your winking gates; 215
And but for our approach, those sleeping stones
That as a waist doth girdle you about,
By the compulsion of their ordinance,
By this [[Time|time]] from their fixèd beds of lime
Had been dishabited, and wide havoc made 220
For bloody power to rush upon your peace.
But on the sight of us your lawful [[King|king]],
Who painfully, with much expedient march,
Have brought a countercheck before your gates
To save unscratched your [[City|city]]’s threatened cheeks,
Behold the French, amazed, vouchsafe a parle; 226
And now instead of bullets wrapped in [[Fire|fire]]
To make a shaking fever in your walls,
They shoot but calm words folded up in smoke
To make a faithless error in your ears; 230
Which trust accordingly, kind citizens,
And let us in, your [[King|king]], whose laboured spirits,
Forwearied in this action of swift [[Speed|speed]],
Craves harbourage within your [[City|city]] walls.
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
When I have said, make answer to us both.
He takes [[Arthur]]’s hand
Lo, in this right hand, whose protection
Is most divinely vowed upon the right
Of him it holds, stands young Plantagenet,
Son to the elder brother of this man
And [[King|king]] o‘er him and all that he enjoys. 240
For this downtrodden equity we tread
In warlike march these greens before your town,
Being no further enemy to you
Than the constraint of hospitable zeal
In the relief of this oppressèd [[Child|child]] 245
Religiously provokes. Be pleased then
To pay that duty which you truly owe
To him that owes it, namely this young prince;
And then our arms, like to a muzzled bear,
Save in aspect, hath all offence sealed up:
Our cannons’ malice vainly shall be spent
Against th’invulnerable clouds of heaven,
And with a blessèd and unvexed retire,
With unhacked swords and helmets all unbruised,
We will bear [[Home|home]] that lusty [[Blood|blood]] again
Which here we came to spout against your town,
And leave your children, wives, and you in peace.
But if you fondly pass our proffered offer,
’Tis not the roundure of your old-faced walls
Can hide you from our messengers of war, 260
Though all these English and their discipline
Were harboured in their rude circumference.
Then tell us, shall your [[City|city]] call us lord
In that behalf which we have challenged it,
Or shall we give the signal to our rage, 265
And stalk in [[Blood|blood]] to our possession?
CITIZEN
In brief, we are the [[King]] of England’s subjects.
For him and in his right we hold this town.
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Acknowledge then the [[King]], and let me in.
CITIZEN
That can we not; but he that proves the [[King|king]], 270
To him will we prove loyal; till that [[Time|time]]
Have we rammed up our gates against the world.
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Doth not the [[Crown|crown]] of England prove the [[King|king]]?
And if not that, I bring you witnesses :
Twice fifteen thousand hearts of England’s breed—
BASTARD (aside) Bastards and else. 276
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
To verify our title with their lives.
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
As many and as [[Well|well]]-born bloods as those—
BASTARD (aside) Some bastards too.
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
Stand in his face to contradict his claim. 280
CITIZEN
Till you compound whose right is worthiest,
We for the worthiest hold the right from both.
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Then God forgive the sin of all those souls
That to their everlasting residence,
Before the dew of evening fall, shall fleet 285
In dreadful trial of our kingdom’s [[King|king]].
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
Amen, Amen! Mount, chevaliers! To arms!
BASTARD
Saint George that swinged the dragon, and e’er since
Sits on’s horseback at mine hostess’ door,
Teach us some fence! (To Austria) Sirrah, were I at
[[Home|home]] 290
At your den, sirrah, with your lioness,
I would set an ox-head to your [[Lion|lion]]’s hide
And make a [[Monster|monster]] of you.
AUSTRIA Peace, no more.
BASTARD
O tremble, for you hear the [[Lion|lion]] roar!
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Up higher to the plain, where we’ll set forth 295
In best appointment all our regiments.
BASTARD
[[Speed]] then, to take advantage of the field.
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
It shall be so, and at the other [[Hill|hill]]
Command the rest to stand. God and our right!
Exeunt ⌈severally⌉ [[King John]] and [[King]] Philip with their powers. The Citizen remains on the walls ⌈Alarum.⌉ Here, after excursions, enter ⌈at one door⌉
FRENCH HERALD
You men of Angers, open wide your gates 300
And let young [[Arthur]] Duke of Brittaine in,
Who by the hand of France this day hath made
Much work for [[Tears|tears]] in many an English mother,
Whose sons lie scattered on the bleeding ground;
Many a widow’s husband grovelling lies, 305
Coldly embracing the discoloured earth;
And victory with little loss doth play
Upon the dancing banners of the French,
Who are at hand, triumphantly displayed,
To enter conquerors, and to proclaim 310
[[Arthur]] of Brittaine England’s [[King|king]] and yours.
Enter ⌈at another door⌉ the English Herald, with a trumpeter
ENGLISH HERALD
Rejoice, you men of Angers, ring your [[Bells|bells]]!
[[King John]], your [[King|king]] and England’s, doth approach,
Commander of this hot malicious day.
Their armours that marched hence so silver-bright 315
Hither return all gilt with Frenchmen’s [[Blood|blood]].
There stuck no plume in any English crest
That is removed by a staff of France;
Our colours do return in those same hands
That did display them when we first marched forth;
And like a jolly troop of huntsmen come 321
Our lusty English, all with purpled hands
Dyed in the dying slaughter of their foes.
Open your gates and give the victors way.
⌈CITIZEN⌉
Heralds, from off our towers we might behold 325
From first to last the onset and retire
Of both your armies, whose equality
By our best eyes cannot be censurèd.
[[Blood]] hath bought [[Blood|blood]] and blows have answered
blows,
Strength matched with strength and power confronted
power. 330
Both are alike, and both alike we like.
One must prove greatest. While they weigh so even,
We hold our town for neither, yet for both.
Enter at one door [[King John]], the Bastard, [[Queen]] [[Eleanor]] and Lady Blanche, with soldiers; at another door [[King]] Philip, Louis the [[Dauphin]], and the Duke of Austria with soldiers
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
France, hast thou yet more [[Blood|blood]] to cast away?
Say, shall the current of our right run on, 335
Whose passage, vexed with thy impediment,
Shall leave his native channel and o’erswell
With course disturbed even thy confining shores,
Unless thou let his silver [[Water|water]] keep
A peaceful progress to the ocean? 340
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
England, thou hast not saved one drop of [[Blood|blood]]
In this hot trial more than we of France;
Rather, lost more. And by this hand I swear,
That sways the earth this climate overlooks,
Before we will lay down our just-borne arms, 345
We’ll put thee down ’gainst whom these arms we
bear,
Or add a royal number to the dead,
Gracing the scroll that tells of this war’s loss
With slaughter coupled to the name of kings.
BASTARD
Ha, majesty! How high thy glory towers 350
When the rich [[Blood|blood]] of kings is set on [[Fire|fire]]!
O, now doth [[Death]] line his dead chaps with steel;
The swords of soldiers are his teeth, his fangs;
And now he feasts, mousing the flesh of men
In undetermined differences of kings.
Why stand these royal fronts amazed thus?
Cry havoc, Kings! Back to the stained field,
You equal potents, fiery-kindled spirits!
Then let confusion of one part confirm
The other’s peace; till then, blows, [[Blood|blood]], and [[Death|death]]!
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Whose party do the townsmen yet admit?
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
Speak, citizens, for England: who’s your [[King|king]]?
⌈CITIZEN⌉
The [[King]] of England, when we know the [[King]].
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
Know him in us, that here hold up his right.
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
In us, that are our own great deputy
And bear possession of our person here,
Lord of our presence, Angers, and of you.
[CITIZEN⌉
A greater power than we denies all this,
And, till it be undoubted, we do lock
Our former scruple in our strong-barred gates, 370
Kinged of our [[Fear|fear]], until our fears resolved
Be by some certain [[King|king]], purged and deposed.
BASTARD
By heaven, these scroyles of Angers flout you, Kings,
And stand securely on their battlements
As in a theatre, whence they gape and point 375
At your industrious scenes and acts of [[Death|death]].
Your royal presences be ruled by me.
Do like the mutines of Jerusalem:
Be friends awhile, and both conjointly bend
Your sharpest deeds of malice on this town. 380
By east and west let France and England mount
Their battering cannon, charged to the mouths,
Till their [[Soul|soul]]-fearing clamours have brawled down
The flinty ribs of this contemptuous [[City|city]].
I’d play incessantly upon these jades,
Even till unfenced desolation
Leave them as naked as the vulgar air.
That done, dissever your united strengths,
And part your mingled colours once again;
Turn face to face, and bloody point to point.
Then in a moment Fortune shall cull forth
Out of one side her happy minion,
To whom in favour she shall give the day,
And kiss him with a glorious victory.
How like you this wild counsel, mighty states?
Smacks it not something of the policy ?
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Now, by the sky that hangs above our heads,
I like it [[Well|well]].-France, shall we knit our powers,
And lay this Angers even with the ground,
Then after fight who shall be [[King|king]] of it?
BASTARD (to [[King]] Philip)
An if thou hast the mettle of a [[King|king]],
Being wronged as we are by this peevish town,
Turn thou the mouth of thy artillery,
As we will ours, against these saucy walls;
And when that we have dashed them to the ground,
Why, then defy each other, and pell-mell
Make work upon ourselves, for heaven or hell.
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
Let it be so.-Say, where will you assault?
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
We from the west will send destruction
Into this [[City|city]]’s bosom.
AUSTRIA I from the north.
[[King|KING]] PHILIP Our thunder from the south
Shall rain their drift of bullets on this town.
BASTARD ⌈to [[King John]]⌉
O prudent discipline! From north to south
Austria and France shoot in each other’s mouth.
I’ll stir them to it. Come, away, away!
⌈CITIZEN⌉
Hear us, great Kings, vouchsafe a while to stay,
And I shall show you peace and fair-faced league.
Win you this [[City|city]] without stroke or wound;
Rescue those breathing lives to die in beds, 420
That here come sacrifices for the field.
Persever not, but hear me, mighty Kings.
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Speak on with favour; we are bent to hear.
⌈CITIZEN⌉
That daughter there of Spain, the Lady Blanche,
Is niece to England. Look upon the years 425
Of Louis the [[Dauphin]] and that lovely maid.
If lusty [[Love|love]] should go in [[Quest|quest]] of [[Beauty|beauty]],
Where should he find it fairer than in Blanche?
If zealous [[Love|love]] should go in search of virtue,
Where should he find it purer than in Blanche? 430
If [[Love|love]] ambitious sought a match of birth,
Whose veins bound richer [[Blood|blood]] than Lady Blanche?
Such as she is in [[Beauty|beauty]], virtue, birth,
Is the young [[Dauphin]] every way complete;
If not complete, O, say he is not she; 435
And she again wants nothing—to name want—
If want it be not that she is not he.
He is the half part of a blessed man,
Left to be finished by such as she;
And she a fair divided excellence, 440
Whose fullness of perfection lies in him.
O, two such silver currents when they join
Do glorify the banks that bound them in,
And two such shores to two such streams made one,
Two such controlling bounds, shall you be, Kings, 445
To these two princes if you marry them.
This [[Union|union]] shall do more than battery can
To our fast-closed gates, for at this match,
With swifter spleen than powder can enforce,
The mouth of passage shall we fling wide ope, 450
And give you entrance. But without this match
The sea enraged is not half so deaf,
Lions more confident, [[Mountains|mountains]] and rocks
More free from motion, no, not [[Death]] himself
In mortal fury half so peremptory, 455
As we to keep this [[City|city]].
BASTARD ⌈aside⌉ Here’s a stay
That shakes the rotten carcass of old [[Death]]
Out of his rags. Here’s a large mouth, indeed,
That spits forth [[Death]] and [[Mountains|mountains]], rocks and seas,
Talks as familiarly of roaring lions 460
As maids of thirteen do of puppy-[[dogs]].
What cannoneer begot this lusty [[Blood|blood]]?
He speaks plain cannon: [[Fire|fire]], and smoke, and bounce;
He gives the bastinado with his tongue;
Our ears are cudgelled; not a word of his 465
But buffets better than a fist of France.
Zounds! I was never so bethumped with words
Since I first called my brother’s father Dad.
[[Queen|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor|ELEANOR]] (aside to [[King John]])
Son, list to this conjunction, make this match,
Give with our niece a dowry large enough; 470
For, by this knot, thou shalt so surely tie
Thy now unsured assurance to the [[Crown|crown]]
That yon green boy shall have no sun to ripe
The bloom that promiseth a mighty fruit.
I see a yielding in the looks of France; 475
Mark how they whisper. Urge them while their souls
Are capable of this [[Ambition|ambition]],
Lest zeal, now melted by the windy breath
Of soft petitions, pity, and remorse,
Cool and congeal again to what it was.
⌈CITIZEN⌉
Why answer not the [[Double|double]] majesties
This friendly treaty of our threatened town?
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
Speak England first, that hath been forward first
To speak unto this [[City|city]]: what say you?
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
If that the [[Dauphin]] there, thy princely son, 485
Can in this book of [[Beauty|beauty]] read ‘I [[Love|love]]’,
Her dowry shall weigh equal with a [[Queen|queen]];
For Anjou and fair Touraine, Maine, Poitou,
And all that we upon this side the sea—
Except this [[City|city]] now by us besieged—490
Find liable to our [[Crown|crown]] and dignity,
Shall gild her bridal bed, and make her rich
In titles, honours, and promotions,
As she in [[Beauty|beauty]], [[Education|education]], [[Blood|blood]],
Holds hand with any princess of the world. 495
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
What sayst thou, boy? Look in the lady’s face.
LOUIS THE [[Dauphin|DAUPHIN]]
I do, my lord, and in her eye I find
A [[Wonder|wonder]], or a wondrous miracle,
The [[Shadow|shadow]] of myself formed in her eye;
Which, being but the [[Shadow|shadow]] of your son, 500
Becomes a sun and makes your son a [[Shadow|shadow]].
I do protest I never loved myself
Till now enfixèd I beheld myself
Drawn in the flattering table of her eye.
He whispers with Blanche
BASTARD (aside)
Drawn in the flattering table of her eye, 505
Hanged in the frowning wrinkle of her brow,
And quartered in her heart: he doth espy
Himself [[Love|love]]’s traitor. This is pity now,
That hanged and drawn and quartered there should be
In such a [[Love|love]] so vile a lout as he. 510
BLANCHE (to Louis the [[Dauphin]])
My uncle’s will in this respect is mine.
If he see aught in you that makes him like,
That anything he sees which moves his liking
I can with ease translate it to my will;
Or if you will, to speak more properly,
I will enforce it easily to my [[Love|love]].
Further I will not flatter you, my lord,
That all I see in you is worthy [[Love|love]],
Than this: that nothing do I see in you,
Though churlish thoughts themselves should be your
judge, 520
That I can find should merit any hate.
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
What say these young ones? What say you, my niece?
BLANCHE
That she is bound in [[Honour|honour]] still to do
What you in wisdom shall vouchsafe to say.
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Speak then, Prince [[Dauphin]], can you [[Love|love]] this lady?
LOUIS THE [[Dauphin|DAUPHIN]]
Nay, ask me if I can refrain from [[Love|love]], 526
For I do [[Love|love]] her most unfeignedly.
[[King John|KING JOHN]]
Then do I give Volquessen, Touraine, Maine,
Poitou, and Anjou, these five provinces,
With her to thee, and this addition more: 530
Full thirty thousand marks of English coin.
Philip of France, if thou be pleased withal,
Command thy son and daughter to join hands.
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
It likes us [[Well|well]].—Young princes, close your hands.
AUSTRIA
And your lips too, for I am [[Well|well]] assured 535
That I did so when I was first assured.
⌈Louis the [[Dauphin]] and Lady Blanche join hands and kiss⌉
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
Now citizens of Angers, ope your gates.
Let in that amity which you have made,
For at Saint Mary’s chapel presently
The rites of [[Marriage|marriage]] shall be solemnized.—540
Is not the Lady [[Constance]] in this troop?
(Aside) I know she is not, for this match made up
Her presence would have interrupted much.
(Aloud) Where is she and her son ? Tell me who knows.
LOUIS THE [[Dauphin|DAUPHIN]]
She is sad and passionate at your highness’ tent. 545
[[King|KING]] PHILIP
And by my [[Faith|faith]] this league that we have made
Will give her sadness very little cure.—
Brother of England, how may we content
This widow lady? In her right we came,
Which we, God knows, have turned another way 550
To our own vantage.
[[King John|KING JOHN]] We will heal up all,
For we’ll create young [[Arthur]] Duke of Brittaine
And [[Earl of Richmond]], and this rich fair town
We make him lord of. Call the Lady [[Constance]].
Some speedy messenger bid her repair
To our solemnity. I trust we shall,
If not fill up the measure of her will,
Yet in some measure satisfy her so
That we shall stop her exclamation.
Go we as [[Well|well]] as haste will suffer us 560
To this unlooked-for, unprepared pomp.
⌈Flourish.⌉ Exeunt all but the Bastard
BASTARD
Mad world, mad kings, mad composition!
John, to stop [[Arthur]]’s title in the whole,
Hath willingly departed with a part;
And France, whose armour [[Conscience|conscience]] buckled on,
Whom zeal and [[Charity|charity]] brought to the field 566
As God’s own soldier, rounded in the ear
With that same purpose-changer, that sly devil,
That broker that still breaks the pate of [[Faith|faith]],
That daily break-vow, he that wins of all, 570
Of kings, of beggars, old men, young men, maids,—
Who having no external thing to lose
But the word ‘maid’, cheats the poor maid of that—
That smooth-faced gentleman, tickling commodity;
Commodity, the bias of the world, 575
The world who of itself is peisèd [[Well|well]],
Made to run even upon even ground,
Till this advantage, this vile-drawing bias,
This sway of motion, this commodity,
Makes it take head from all indifferency, 580
From all direction, purpose, course, intent;
And this same bias, this commodity,
This bawd, this broker, this all-changing word,
Clapped on the outward eye of fickle France,
Hath drawn him from his own determined aid, 585
From a resolved and honourable war,
To a most base and vile-concluded peace.
And why rail I on this commodity?
But for because he hath not wooed me yet—
Not that I have the power to clutch my hand 590
When his fair angels would salute my palm,
But for my hand, as unattempted yet,
Like a poor beggar raileth on the rich.
[[Well]], whiles I am a beggar I will rail,
And say there is no sin but to be rich, 595
And being rich, my virtue then shall be
To say there is no vice but beggary.
Since kings break [[Faith|faith]] upon commodity,
Gain, be my lord, for I will worship thee. Exit

### Atto 2, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lady [[Constance]], [[Arthur]] Duke of Brittaine, and the [[Earl of Salisbury]] |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | (to Salisbury)<br>Gone to be married? Gone to swear a peace?<br>False [[Blood\|blood]] to false [[Blood\|blood]] joined! Gone to be friends?<br>Shall Louis have Blanche, and Blanche those<br>provinces?<br>It is not so, thou hast misspoke, misheard.<br>Be [[Well\|well]] advised, tell o‘er thy tale again.<br>It cannot be, thou dost but say ’tis so.<br>I trust I may not trust thee, for thy word<br>Is but the vain breath of a [[Common Man\|common man]].<br>Believe me, I do not believe thee, man;<br>I have a [[King\|king]]’s oath to the contrary. 10<br>Thou shalt be punished for thus frighting me;<br>For I am sick and capable of fears;<br>Oppressed with wrongs, and therefore full of fears;<br>A widow husbandless, subject to fears;<br>A woman naturally born to fears;<br>And though thou now confess thou didst but jest,<br>With my vexed spirits I cannot take a truce,<br>But they will quake and tremble all this day.<br>What dost thou mean by shaking of thy head?<br>Why dost thou look so sadly on my son? 20<br>What means that hand upon that breast of thine?<br>Why holds thine eye that lamentable rheum,<br>Like a proud [[River\|river]] peering o’er his bounds?<br>Be these sad signs confirmers of thy words?<br>Then speak again—not all thy former tale,<br>But this one word: whether thy tale be true. |
| SALISBURY | As true as I believe you think them false<br>That give you cause to prove my saying true. |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | O, if thou teach me to believe this sorrow,<br>Teach thou this sorrow how to make me die;<br>And let belief and life encounter so<br>As doth the fury of two desperate men<br>Which in the very meeting fall and die.<br>Louis marry Blanche! (To [[Arthur]]) O boy, then where<br>art thou?<br>France friend with England!—What becomes of me?<br>(To Salisbury) Fellow, be gone, I cannot brook thy<br>sight; 36<br>This news hath made thee a most ugly man. |
| SALISBURY | What other harm have I, good lady, done,<br>But spoke the harm that is by others done? |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | Which harm within itself so heinous is 40<br>As it makes harmful all that speak of it. |
| [[Arthur\|ARTHUR]] | I do beseech you, madam, be content. |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | If thou that bidd‘st me be content wert grim,<br>Ugly and sland’rous to thy mother’s womb,<br>Full of unpleasing blots and sightless stains,<br>Lame, foolish, crooked, swart, prodigious,<br>Patched with foul moles and eye-offending marks,<br>I would not care, I then would be content,<br>For then I should not [[Love\|love]] thee, no, nor thou<br>Become thy great birth, nor deserve a [[Crown\|crown]].<br>But thou art fair, and at thy birth, dear boy,<br>[[Nature]] and Fortune joined to make thee great.<br>Of [[Nature]]’s gifts thou mayst with lilies boast,<br>And with the half-blown [[Rose\|rose]]. But Fortune, O,<br>She is corrupted, changed, and won from thee;<br>Sh’adulterates hourly with thine uncle John,<br>And with her golden hand hath plucked on France<br>To tread down fair respect of sovereignty,<br>And made his majesty the bawd to theirs.<br>France is a bawd to Fortune and [[King John]],<br>That strumpet Fortune, that usurping John.<br>(To Salisbury) Tell me, thou fellow, is not France<br>forsworn ?<br>Envenom him with words, or get thee gone<br>And leave those woes [[Alone\|alone]], which I [[Alone\|alone]]<br>Am bound to underbear. |
| SALISBURY | Pardon me, madam,<br>I may not go without you to the Kings. |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | Thou mayst, thou shalt; I will not go with thee.<br>I will instruct my sorrows to be proud,<br>For grief is proud and makes his owner stoop. |
| *(didascalia)* | She sits upon the ground |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | To me and to the state of my great grief 70<br>Let kings assemble, for my grief’s so great<br>That no supporter but the huge firm earth<br>Can hold it up. Here I and sorrows sit;<br>Here is my throne; bid kings come bow to it. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Salisbury and [[Arthur]] |
| *(didascalia)* | 3.1 Flourish. Enter [[King John]] and [[King]] Philip hand in hand; Louis the [[Dauphin]] and Lady Blanche, married; [[Queen]] [[Eleanor]], the Bastard, and the Duke of Austria |
| [[King\|KING]] PHILIP | (to Blanche)<br>’Tis true, fair daughter, and this blessèd day<br>Ever in France shall be kept festival.<br>To solemnize this day, the glorious sun<br>Stays in his course and plays the alchemist,<br>Turning with splendour of his precious eye 5<br>The meagre cloddy earth to glittering gold.<br>The yearly course that brings this day about<br>Shall never see it but a holy day. |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | (rising)<br>A wicked day, and not a holy day!<br>What hath this day deserved? What hath it done,<br>That it in golden letters should be set<br>Among the high tides in the calendar?<br>Nay, rather turn this day out of the week,<br>This day of shame, oppression, perjury.<br>Or if it must stand still, let wives with [[Child\|child]] 15<br>Pray that their burdens may not fall this day,<br>Lest that their hopes prodigiously be crossed;<br>But on this day let seamen [[Fear\|fear]] no wreck;<br>No bargains break that are not this day made;<br>This day all things begun come to ill end, 20<br>Yea, [[Faith\|faith]] itself to hollow falsehood change. |
| [[King\|KING]] PHILIP | By heaven, lady, you shall have no cause<br>To curse the fair proceedings of this day.<br>Have I not pawned to you my majesty? |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | You have beguiled me with a counterfeit 25<br>Resembling majesty, which being touched and tried<br>Proves valueless. You are forsworn, forsworn.<br>You came in arms to spill mine enemies’ [[Blood\|blood]],<br>But now in arms you strengthen it with yours.<br>The grappling vigour and rough frown of war 30<br>Is cold in amity and painted peace,<br>And our oppression hath made up this league.<br>Arm, arm, you heavens, against these perjured Kings!<br>A widow cries, be husband to me, God!<br>Let not the hours of this ungodly day 35<br>Wear out the day in peace, but ere sun set<br>Set armed discord ’twixt these perjured Kings.<br>Hear me, O hear me! |
| AUSTRIA | Lady [[Constance]], peace. |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | War, war, no peace! Peace is to me a war.<br>O Limoges, O Austria, thou dost shame 40<br>That bloody spoil. Thou slave, thou wretch, thou<br>coward!<br>Thou little valiant, great in villainy;<br>Thou ever strong upon the stronger side;<br>Thou Fortune’s champion, that dost never fight<br>But when her humorous ladyship is by 45<br>To teach thee safety. Thou art perjured too,<br>And sooth‘st up [[Greatness\|greatness]]. What a [[Fool\|fool]] art thou,<br>A ramping [[Fool\|fool]], to brag and stamp, and swear<br>Upon my party! Thou cold-blooded slave,<br>Hast thou not spoke like thunder on my side, 50<br>Been sworn my soldier, bidding me depend<br>Upon thy [[Stars\|stars]], thy fortune, and thy strength?<br>And dost thou now fall over to my foes?<br>Thou wear a [[Lion\|lion]]’s hide! Doff it, for shame,<br>And hang a calf’s-skin on those recreant limbs. 55 |
| AUSTRIA | O, that a man should speak those words to me! |
| BASTARD | And hang a calf’s-skin on those recreant limbs. |
| AUSTRIA | Thou dar’st not say so, [[Villain\|villain]], for thy life. |
| BASTARD | And hang a calf’s-skin on those recreant limbs. |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | (to the Bastard)<br>We like not this. Thou dost forget thyself. 60 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Cardinal Pandolf |
| [[King\|KING]] PHILIP | Here comes the holy legate of the Pope. |
| PANDOLF | Hail, you anointed deputies of God.—<br>To thee, [[King John]], my holy errand is.<br>I Pandolf, of fair Milan Cardinal,<br>And from Pope [[Innocent]] the legate here, 65<br>Do in his name religiously demand<br>Why thou against the Church, our Holy Mother,<br>So wilfully dost spurn, and force perforce<br>Keep Stephen Langton, chosen Archbishop<br>Of Canterbury, from that holy see. 70<br>This, in our foresaid Holy Father’s name,<br>Pope [[Innocent]], I do demand of thee. |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | What earthy name to interrogatories<br>Can task the free breath of a sacred [[King\|king]]?<br>Thou canst not, Cardinal, devise a name 75<br>So slight, unworthy, and ridiculous<br>To charge me to an answer, as the Pope.<br>Tell him this tale, and from the mouth of England<br>Add thus much more: that no Italian priest<br>Shall tithe or toll in our dominions; 80<br>But as we, under God, are supreme head,<br>So, under him, that great supremacy<br>Where we do reign we will [[Alone\|alone]] uphold<br>Without th’assistance of a mortal hand.<br>So tell the Pope, all reverence set apart<br>To him and his usurped authority. |
| [[King\|KING]] PHILIP | Brother of England, you blaspheme in this. |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | Though you and all the kings of Christendom<br>Are led so grossly by this meddling priest,<br>Dreading the curse that money may buy out, 90<br>And by the merit of vile gold, dross, [[Dust\|dust]],<br>Purchase corrupted pardon of a man,<br>Who in that sale sells pardon from himself;<br>Though you and all the rest so grossly led<br>This juggling witchcraft with revenue cherish; 95<br>Yet I [[Alone\|alone]], [[Alone\|alone]] do me oppose<br>Against the Pope, and count his friends my foes. |
| PANDOLF | Then by the lawful power that I have<br>Thou shalt stand cursed and excommunicate;<br>And blessed shall he be that doth revolt 100<br>From his allegiance to an heretic;<br>And meritorious shall that hand be called,<br>Canonized and worshipped as a saint,<br>That takes away by any secret course<br>Thy hateful life. |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | O lawful let it be 105<br>That I have room with Rome to curse awhile.<br>Good Father Cardinal, cry thou ’Amen’<br>To my keen curses, for without my wrong<br>There is no tongue hath power to curse him right. |
| PANDOLF | There’s law and warrant, lady, for my curse. 110 |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | And for mine too. When law can do no right,<br>Let it be lawful that law bar no wrong.<br>Law cannot give my [[Child\|child]] his kingdom here,<br>For he that holds his kingdom holds the law.<br>Therefore, since law itself is perfect wrong,<br>How can the law forbid my tongue to curse? |
| PANDOLF | Philip of France, on peril of a curse,<br>Let go the hand of that arch-heretic,<br>And raise the power of France upon his head,<br>Unless he do submit himself to Rome. 120 |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor\|ELEANOR]] | Look’st thou pale, France? Do not let go thy hand. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] to [[King John]] |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor\|ELEANOR]] | Look to it, devil, lest that France repent,<br>And by disjoining hands hell lose a [[Soul\|soul]]. |
| AUSTRIA | [[King]] Philip, listen to the Cardinal. |
| BASTARD | And hang a calf’s-skin on his recreant limbs. 125 |
| AUSTRIA | [[Well]], ruffian, I must pocket up these wrongs,<br>Because— |
| BASTARD | Your breeches best may carry them. |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | Philip, what sayst thou to the Cardinal? |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | What should he say, but as the Cardinal? |
| LOUIS THE [[Dauphin\|DAUPHIN]] | Bethink you, Father, for the difference 130<br>Is purchase of a heavy curse from Rome,<br>Or the light loss of England for a friend.<br>Forgo the easier. |
| BLANCHE | That’s the curse of Rome. |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | O Louis, stand fast; the devil tempts thee here<br>In likeness of a new untrimmèd bride. 135 |
| BLANCHE | The Lady [[Constance]] speaks not from her [[Faith\|faith]],<br>But from her need. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] to [[King]] Philip O if thou grant my need, |
| BLANCHE | Which only lives but by the [[Death\|death]] of [[Faith\|faith]],<br>That need must needs infer this principle:<br>That [[Faith\|faith]] would live again by [[Death\|death]] of need. 140<br>O, then tread down my need, and [[Faith\|faith]] mounts up;<br>Keep my need up, and [[Faith\|faith]] is trodden down. |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | The [[King]] is moved, and answers not to this. |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | (to [[King]] Philip)<br>O, be removed from him, and answer [[Well\|well]]. |
| AUSTRIA | Do so, [[King]] Philip, hang no more in [[Doubt\|doubt]]. 145 |
| BASTARD | Hang nothing but a calf’s-skin, most sweet lout. |
| [[King\|KING]] PHILIP | I am perplexed, and know not what to say. |
| PANDOLF | What canst thou say but will perplex thee more,<br>If thou stand excommunicate and cursed? |
| [[King\|KING]] PHILIP | Good Reverend Father, make my person yours, 150<br>And tell me how you would bestow yourself.<br>This royal hand and mine are newly knit,<br>And the conjunction of our inward souls<br>Married in league, coupled and linked together<br>With all religious strength of sacred vows; 155<br>The latest breath that gave the sound of words<br>Was deep-sworn [[Faith\|faith]], peace, amity, true [[Love\|love]],<br>Between our kingdoms and our royal selves;<br>And even before this truce, but new before,<br>No longer than we [[Well\|well]] could wash our hands 160<br>To clap this royal bargain up of peace,<br>God knows, they were besmeared and over-stained<br>With slaughter’s pencil, where [[Revenge]] did paint<br>The fearful difference of incensed kings;<br>And shall these hands, so lately purged of [[Blood\|blood]], 165<br>So newly joined in [[Love\|love]], so strong in both,<br>Unyoke this seizure and this kind regreet,<br>Play fast and loose with [[Faith\|faith]], so jest with heaven,<br>Make such unconstant children of ourselves,<br>As now again to snatch our palm from palm, 170<br>Unswear [[Faith\|faith]] sworn, and on the [[Marriage\|marriage]]-bed<br>Of smiling peace to march a bloody host,<br>And make a riot on the gentle brow<br>Of true sincerity? O holy sir,<br>My Reverend Father, let it not be so. 175<br>Out of your grace, devise, ordain, impose<br>Some gentle order, and then we shall be blessed<br>To do your pleasure and continue friends. |
| PANDOLF | All form is formless, order orderless,<br>Save what is opposite to England’s [[Love\|love]].<br>Therefore to arms, be champion of our Church,<br>Or let the Church, our mother, breathe her curse,<br>A mother’s curse, on her revolting son.<br>France, thou mayst hold a serpent by the tongue,<br>A crazed [[Lion\|lion]] by the mortal paw, 185<br>A fasting tiger safer by the tooth,<br>Than keep in peace that hand which thou dost hold. |
| [[King\|KING]] PHILIP | I may disjoin my hand, but not my [[Faith\|faith]]. |
| PANDOLF | So mak‘st thou [[Faith\|faith]] an enemy to [[Faith\|faith]],<br>And like a civil war, sett’st oath to oath, 190<br>Thy tongue against thy tongue. O, let thy vow,<br>First made to heaven, first be to heaven performed;<br>That is, to be the champion of our Church.<br>What since thou swor‘st is sworn against thyself,<br>And may not be performèd by thyself; 195<br>For that which thou hast sworn to do amiss<br>Is not amiss when it is truly done;<br>And being not done where doing tends to ill,<br>The [[Truth\|truth]] is then most done not doing it.<br>The better act of purposes mistook 200<br>Is to mistake again; though indirect,<br>Yet indirection thereby grows direct,<br>And falsehood falsehood cures, as [[Fire\|fire]] cools [[Fire\|fire]] |
| *(didascalia)* | Within the scorchèd veins of one new burned. |
| PANDOLF | It is religion that doth make vows kept;<br>But thou hast sworn against religion;<br>By what thou swear’st, against the thing thou<br>swear‘st;<br>And mak’st an oath the surety for thy troth:<br>Against an oath, the [[Truth\|truth]]. Thou art unsure<br>To swear: swear’st only not to be forsworn—210<br>Else what a mockery should it be to swear!—<br>But thou dost swear only to be forsworn,<br>And most forsworn to keep what thou dost swear;<br>Therefore thy later vows against thy first<br>Is in thyself rebellion to thyself, 215<br>And better conquest never canst thou make<br>Than arm thy constant and thy nobler parts<br>Against these giddy loose suggestions;<br>Upon which better part our prayers come in<br>If thou vouchsafe them. But if not, then know 220<br>The peril of our curses light on thee<br>So heavy as thou shalt not shake them off,<br>But in [[Despair\|despair]] die under their black weight. |
| AUSTRIA | Rebellion, flat rebellion! |
| BASTARD | Wilt not be?<br>Will not a calf’s-skin stop that mouth of thine? 225 |
| LOUIS THE [[Dauphin\|DAUPHIN]] | Father, to arms! |
| BLANCHE | Upon thy wedding day?<br>Against the [[Blood\|blood]] that thou hast married ?<br>What, shall our feast be kept with slaughtered men?<br>Shall braying trumpets and loud churlish drums,<br>Clamours of hell, be measures to our pomp? 230<br>She kneels<br>O husband, hear me ! Ay, alack, how new<br>Is ‘husband’ in my mouth! Even for that name<br>Which till this [[Time\|time]] my tongue did ne’er pronounce,<br>Upon my knee I beg, go not to arms<br>Against mine uncle. |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | (kneeling) O, upon my knee 235<br>Made hard with kneeling, I do pray to thee,<br>Thou virtuous [[Dauphin]], alter not the doom<br>Forethought by heaven. |
| BLANCHE | (to Louis the [[Dauphin]])<br>Now shall I see thy [[Love\|love]]: what motive may<br>Be stronger with thee than the name of wife? 240 |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | That which upholdeth him that thee upholds:<br>His [[Honour\|honour]].—O thine [[Honour\|honour]], Louis, thine [[Honour\|honour]]! |
| LOUIS THE [[Dauphin\|DAUPHIN]] | (to [[King]] Philip)<br>I muse your majesty doth seem so cold<br>When such profound respects do pull you on. |
| PANDOLF | I will denounce a curse upon his head. 245 |
| [[King\|KING]] PHILIP | Thou shalt not need.—England, I will fall from thee. |
| *(didascalia)* | He takes his hand from [[King John]]’s hand. Blanche and [[Constance]] rise |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | O, fair return of banished majesty! |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor\|ELEANOR]] | O, foul revolt of French inconstancy I |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | France, thou shalt rue this hour within this hour. |
| BASTARD | Old [[Time]] the [[Clock\|clock]]-setter, that bald sexton [[Time]], 250<br>Is it as he will?—[[Well]] then, France shall rue. |
| BLANCHE | The sun’s o’ercast with [[Blood\|blood]]; fair day, adieu!<br>Which is the side that I must go withal?<br>I am with both, each army hath a hand,<br>And in their rage, I having hold of both, 255<br>They whirl asunder and dismember me.<br>Husband, I cannot pray that thou mayst win.—<br>Uncle, I needs must pray that thou mayst lose.—<br>Father, I may not wish the fortune thine.—<br>Grandam, I will not wish thy wishes thrive.<br>Whoever wins, on that side shall I lose,<br>Assured loss before the match be played. |
| LOUIS THE [[Dauphin\|DAUPHIN]] | Lady, with me, with me thy fortune lies. |
| BLANCHE | There where my fortune lives, there my life dies. |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | (to the Bastard)<br>Cousin, go draw our puissance together.— |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit the Bastard |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | France, I am burned up with inflaming wrath,<br>A rage whose heat hath this condition:<br>That nothing can allay, nothing but [[Blood\|blood]],<br>The [[Blood\|blood]], and dearest-valued [[Blood\|blood]], of France. |
| [[King\|KING]] PHILIP | Thy rage shall burn thee up, and thou shalt turn 270<br>To ashes ere our [[Blood\|blood]] shall quench that [[Fire\|fire]].<br>Look to thyself, thou art in jeopardy. |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | No more than he that threats.—To arms let’s hie! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt severally |

### Atto 3, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum; excursions. Enter the Bastard, with the Duke of Austria’s head |
| BASTARD | Now, by my life, this day grows wondrous hot;<br>Some airy devil hovers in the sky<br>And pours down mischief. Austria’s head lie there,<br>While Philip breathes. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[King John]], [[Arthur]] Duke of Brittaine, and [[Hubert]] |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | [[Hubert]], keep this boy.—Philip, make up! 5<br>My mother is assailed in our tent,<br>And ta’en I [[Fear\|fear]]. |
| BASTARD | My lord, I rescued her;<br>Her highness is in safety; [[Fear\|fear]] you not.<br>But on, my liege, for very little pains<br>Will bring this labour to an happy end. 10 |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[King John]] and the Bastard at one door, [[Hubert]] and [[Arthur]] at another door |

### Atto 3, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum; excursions; retreat. Enter [[King John]], [[Queen]] [[Eleanor]], [[Arthur]] Duke of Brittaine, the Bastard, [[Hubert]], lords, with soldiers |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | (to [[Queen]] [[Eleanor]])<br>So shall it be; your grace shall stay behind<br>So strongly guarded. (To [[Arthur]]) Cousin, look not sad;<br>Thy grandam loves thee, and thy uncle will<br>As dear be to thee as thy father was. |
| [[Arthur\|ARTHUR]] | O, this will make my mother die with grief. 5 |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | (to the Bastard)<br>Cousin, away for England! Haste before,<br>And ere our coming, see thou shake the bags<br>Of hoarding abbots. The fat ribs of peace<br>Must by the hungry now be fed upon.<br>Imprisoned angels set at liberty. 10<br>Use our commission in his utmost force. |
| BASTARD | Bell, book, and candle shall not drive me back<br>When gold and silver becks me to come on.<br>I leave your highness.—Grandam, I will pray,<br>If ever I remember to be holy,<br>For your fair safety. So I kiss your hand. |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor\|ELEANOR]] | Farewell, gentle cousin. |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | Coz, farewell. Exit the Bastard |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor\|ELEANOR]] | Come hither, little kinsman. Hark, a word.<br>She takes [[Arthur]] aside |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | Come hither, [[Hubert]].<br>He takes [[Hubert]] aside<br>O my gentle [[Hubert]],<br>We owe thee much. Within this wall of flesh 20<br>There is a [[Soul\|soul]] counts thee her creditor,<br>And with advantage means to pay thy [[Love\|love]];<br>And, my good friend, thy voluntary oath<br>Lives in this bosom, dearly cherished.<br>Give me thy hand.<br>He takes [[Hubert]]’s hand<br>I had a thing to say, 25<br>But I will fit it with some better tune.<br>By heaven, [[Hubert]], I am almost ashamed<br>To say what good respect I have of thee. |
| [[Hubert\|HUBERT]] | I am much bounden to your majesty. |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | Good friend, thou hast no cause to say so yet, 30<br>But thou shalt have; and creep [[Time\|time]] ne‘er so slow,<br>Yet it shall come for me to do thee good.<br>I had a thing to say—but let it go.<br>The sun is in the heaven, and the proud day,<br>Attended with the pleasures of the world, 35<br>Is all too wanton and too full of gauds<br>To give me audience. If the midnight bell<br>Did with his iron tongue and brazen mouth |
| *(didascalia)* | Sound on into the drowsy race of [[Night\|night]]; |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | If this same were a churchyard where we stand, 40<br>And thou possessed with a thousand wrongs;<br>Or if that surly spirit, [[Melancholy\|melancholy]],<br>Had baked thy [[Blood\|blood]] and made it heavy, thick,<br>Which else runs tickling up and down the veins,<br>Making that idiot, laughter, keep men’s eyes 45<br>And strain their cheeks to idle merriment—<br>A passion hateful to my purposes—<br>Or if that thou couldst see me without eyes,<br>Hear me without thine ears, and make reply<br>Without a tongue, using conceit [[Alone\|alone]],<br>Without eyes, ears, and harmful sound of words;<br>Then in despite of broad-eyed watchful day<br>I would into thy bosom pour my thoughts.<br>But, ah, I will not. Yet I [[Love\|love]] thee [[Well\|well]],<br>And by my troth, I think thou lov’st me [[Well\|well]]. |
| [[Hubert\|HUBERT]] | So [[Well\|well]] that what you bid me undertake,<br>Though that my [[Death\|death]] were adjunct to my act,<br>By heaven, I would do it. |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | Do not I know thou wouldst?<br>Good [[Hubert]], [[Hubert]], [[Hubert]], throw thine eye<br>On yon young boy. I’ll tell thee what, my friend,<br>He is a very serpent in my way,<br>And wheresoe’er this foot of mine doth tread,<br>He lies before me. Dost thou understand me?<br>Thou art his keeper. |
| [[Hubert\|HUBERT]] | And I’ll keep him so<br>That he shall not offend your majesty. 65 |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | [[Death]]. |
| [[Hubert\|HUBERT]] | My lord. |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | A [[Grave\|grave]]. |
| [[Hubert\|HUBERT]] | He shall not live. |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | Enough.<br>I could be merry now. [[Hubert]], I [[Love\|love]] thee.<br>[[Well]], I’ll not say what I intend for thee.<br>Remember. (To [[Queen]] [[Eleanor]]) Madam, fare you [[Well\|well]].<br>I’ll send those powers o’er to your majesty. 70 |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] [[Eleanor\|ELEANOR]] | My blessing go with thee. |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | (to [[Arthur]]) For England, cousin, go.<br>[[Hubert]] shall be your man, attend on you<br>With all true duty.—On toward Calais, ho! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Queen]] [[Eleanor]], attended, at one door, the rest at another door |

### Atto 3, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[King]] Philip, Louis the [[Dauphin]], Cardinal Pandolf, and attendants |
| [[King\|KING]] PHILIP | So, by a roaring tempest on the flood,<br>A whole armada of convicted sail<br>Is scattered and disjoined from fellowship. |
| PANDOLF | [[Courage]] and comfort; all shall yet go [[Well\|well]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] PHILIP | What can go [[Well\|well]] when we have run so ill?<br>Are we not beaten? Is not Angers lost,<br>[[Arthur]] ta‘en prisoner, divers dear friends slain,<br>And bloody England into England gone,<br>O’erbearing interruption, spite of France? |
| LOUIS THE [[Dauphin\|DAUPHIN]] | What he hath won, that hath he fortified. 10<br>So hot a [[Speed\|speed]], with such advice disposed,<br>Such temperate order in so fierce a cause,<br>Doth want example. Who hath read or heard<br>Of any kindred action like to this? |
| [[King\|KING]] PHILIP | [[Well]] could I bear that England had this praise, 15<br>So we could find some pattern of our shame. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Constance]], distracted, with her hair about her ears |
| [[King\|KING]] PHILIP | Look who comes here! A [[Grave\|grave]] unto a [[Soul\|soul]],<br>Holding th’eternal spirit against her will<br>In the vile [[Prison\|prison]] of afflicted breath.—<br>I prithee, lady, go away with me. 20 |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | Lo, now, now see the issue of your peace! |
| [[King\|KING]] PHILIP | Patience, good lady; comfort, gentle [[Constance]]. |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | No, I defy all counsel, all redress,<br>But that which ends all counsel, true redress:<br>[[Death]], [[Death]], O amiable, lovely [[Death]]! 25<br>Thou odoriferous stench, sound rottenness!<br>Arise forth from the couch of lasting [[Night\|night]],<br>Thou hate and terror to prosperity,<br>And I will kiss thy detestable bones,<br>And put my eyeballs in thy vaulty brows, 30<br>And ring these fingers with thy household worms,<br>And stop this gap of breath with fulsome [[Dust\|dust]],<br>And be a carrion [[Monster\|monster]] like thyself.<br>Come grin on me, and I will think thou smil’st,<br>And buss thee as thy wife. Misery’s [[Love\|love]], 35<br>O, come to me! |
| [[King\|KING]] PHILIP | O fair affliction, peace I |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | No, no, I will not, having breath to cry.<br>O, that my tongue were in the thunder’s mouth!<br>Then with a passion would I shake the world,<br>And rouse from [[Sleep\|sleep]] that fell anatomy,<br>Which cannot hear a lady’s feeble voice,<br>Which scorns a modern invocation. |
| PANDOLF | Lady, you utter [[Madness\|madness]], and not sorrow. |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | Thou art not holy to belie me so.<br>I am not mad: this hair I tear is mine;<br>My name is [[Constance]]; I was Geoffrey’s wife;<br>Young [[Arthur]] is my son; and he is lost.<br>I am not mad; I would to God I were,<br>For then ’tis like I should forget myself.<br>O,if I could, what grief should I forget ! 50<br>Preach some [[philosophy]] to make me mad,<br>And thou shalt be canonized, Cardinal.<br>For, being not mad, but sensible of grief,<br>My reasonable part produces reason<br>How I may be delivered of these woes, 55<br>And teaches me to kill or hang myself.<br>If I were mad I should forget my son,<br>Or madly think a babe of clouts were he.<br>I am not mad; too [[Well\|well]], too [[Well\|well]] I feel<br>The different plague of each calamity. 60 |
| [[King\|KING]] PHILIP | Bind up those tresses. O,what [[Love\|love]] I note<br>In the fair multitude of those her hairs!<br>Where but by chance a silver drop hath fallen,<br>Even to that drop ten thousand wiry friends<br>Do glue themselves in sociable grief, 65<br>Like true, inseparable, faithful loves,<br>Sticking together in calamity. |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | To England, if you will. |
| [[King\|KING]] PHILIP | Bind up your hairs. |
| [[Constance\|CONSTANCE]] | Yes, that I will.
